(100)

One can immediately notice that directly going from Eq. (
99
) to Eq. (
100
) involves interactions between left and right flags separated by
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

. In the worst case, the first
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

in Eq. (
99
) interacts with the first
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

in Eq. (
100
). This is highly non-local. Thus, once the ‘downward’ phase is finished and we reach the end of a block, we will ‘move’ the state in the current block to the next one qudit by qudit: we first move the first particle from the current block all the way through the other particles, and place it in the first position of the next block, reinitializing it. Then we repeat this with each one of the particles as shown in steps 4 - 22 in
fig.

4
. By the end of this process, the whole state is reinitialized in Eq. (
100
) and we can proceed with the next layer of gates.

3.1
One-dimensional transition rules and propagation terms

We first define the rules that govern the transition of states from one to another in our construction. Then we demonstrate a full cycle on 4 qubits starting from a gate on one block up until reinitializing in the second block. We define a block as a sub-chain of length
n^{\prime}-1
, where
n^{\prime}
is the number of qubits in the MA
{}_{\text{q}}
verification circuit as described in
section

2.1
. There are a total of
R
such blocks (where
R
is the number of layers in the original MA verification circuit) constituting the entire one-dimensional chain of size
(n^{\prime}-1)R
.

Inactive states

Active states

{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

: Stores 2-qubit data when
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

is inactive.

{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

: Gate flag (tracks gate application).

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

: Qubits on the right of the active site.

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

: Right-moving flag.

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

: Qubits on the left of the active site.

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

: Left-moving flag.

\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

: Unborn (to right of all qubits).

\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

: Turning flag (changes sweep direction).

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

: Dead (to left of all qubits).

Table 2:
Classification of states into ‘active’ and ‘inactive’ states. Both forward and backward transition of states is governed primarily by the active state. The two-local Hamiltonian terms applied at each time step are either on the left or on the right (neighbouring inactive state) of the active state. Neighbouring inactive states together with the current active state determine the transition to the next configuration.

Remark 18

(Notation)
.

Note that
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

, while pointing in only one horizontal direction, still carries two-dimensional data. The right-pointing shape is chosen for clarity.

In the case of left and right moving flags, it is the next particle in the same direction. In case of
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

it is always right. As for the turning flag, note that we do not need the turn flag on the right turn, as opposed to
Ref.
[
1
]
. The reason we do not need a
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

flag on the right turn is because the incoming right flag is already two dimensional interacting with the particle on its right —
\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

— which is one-dimensional to convert into a
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

and
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

, which is the same total dimension of three. However, in the left sweep the one-dimensional flag,
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

interacting with
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

(another one-dimensional flag) cannot give
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

and
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

(two-dimensional),
because that would map two-dimensional input to three-dimensional output. Each active site in a given configuration has a unique forward and backward transition rule as can be noted in the propagation rules. All particles except
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

,
\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

, and
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

are data-carrying particles — they store qubit data. Data carrying particles denoted by two shapes in a box are four-dimensional, and those with only one shape in a box are two dimensional.

We now turn to discussing the one-dimensional propagation rules.

Remark 19

(Notation)
.

\big\|
denotes that the rule holds for both with and without a boundary. For example the
first rule in Rule 4 (left turn) holds for with and without boundary.

Using these, we can define
H_{\rm Prop}
analogously to the two-dimensional case as in Section
2.4
. Note that these terms are still stoquastic because the terms with
-U_{l}
are stoquastic due to
U_{l}
being Toffolis. And all the remaining terms are projections or have non-positive off-diagonals.

Rule 1:

22.

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

.

Figure 4:
A cycle for the forward and the reinitialization phase for 4 qubits. It takes
(n^{\prime}-1)(2n^{\prime}-1)
time steps for a
single cycle, where
n^{\prime}
is
the
number of qubits in the verification circuit.
In total, it takes

T=(2n^{\prime}-1)(n^{\prime}-1)(R-1)+(n^{\prime}-1)
, where
R
is the total number of layers in the verification circuit.

Note that in the simplest case, one can translate the one-dimensional construction
of Ref.
[
1
]
and simply translate the one qubit left and right flags to the two qubit ones (similar to the two-dimensional construction). This would require us to to have a four-dimensional left and right flags each, denoting whether the inactive state
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

is on the left or the right of the active qubit. Right away, this introduces another 8 dimensions, 4 for left and right each. However, we can do better by exploiting the structure of our one-dimensional grid. Note that
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

always stays at the boundary — either on its left or on its right. Whenever
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

is on the left of the boundary, it is always on the left of the active particle and similarly for the right hand side. Thus by looking at the boundary on the left or the right of
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

, we can determine whether
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

is on the left of the active particle or on its right — thus acting as a left and right flag without needing to introduce extra 8 dimensions for the two flags. And whenever the state is
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

, then it is the active site itself. While this construction saves on the dimensionality of the particles, one can trade this with block length by decreasing the current block length of
n^{\prime}-1
to
n^{\prime}/2
(same as in the two-dimensional case) and increasing the particle dimension of each particle. One might notice that this insight can potentially be used to reduce the dimension in the two-dimensional version of the problem. We leave this as a clue for possible future work.

3.2
Illegal configurations and penalty terms in one spatial dimension

Like the two-dimensional case, there is a possibility that we might be in an illegal state that does not correspond to a valid propagation from a valid starting state. We give an exhaustive list of all such illegal states in Table
4
. While the table of
one-dimensional prohibited states looks involved, it can simply be constructed by considering the allowed states on the left and right of each states, both with and without boundary by following the propagation cycle. However, there are configurations that do not appear in the configuration cycle and should still be penalized, which are shown in the final two rows of Table
4
. Since all such states are nearest-neighbour (2-local) states, they can be penalized by the penalty Hamiltonian
H_{\rm Penalty}=\sum_{i\in{\rm Illegal}}\Pi_{i}
, where the sum is over all the prohibited states in Table
4
.

Unlike the two-dimensional case, however, we shall encounter illegal configurations that cannot have a two-local check like the ones listed in Table
4
, and therefore are not penalized by
H_{\rm Penalty}
. This problem also appeared in the the proof of QMA-hardness of general local Hamiltonians on a line
[
1
]
, and it arises because resetting one block requires moving all the states one-by-one from one block to the next. Let us first define the state configurations that appear in the 1D construction and prove some essential facts about them.

Definition 20

(Qudit strings)
.

For all valid configurations on the one-dimensional grid let the (
qudits
) strings be defined as any configuration of shapes between dead

(101)

where the number of
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

and
\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

is not necessarily the same on the left and right, respectively.

Fact 21

(Legal qudit length)
.

All legal (
qudits
) strings where the active state is either
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

or
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

have length
n^{\prime}-1
. Moreover,
all legal (
qudits
) strings where the active state is
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

or
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

have length
n^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Firstly, recall that the initial length of a block (from one boundary to the next) is
n^{\prime}-1
, where
n^{\prime}
is the total number of qubits in the verification circuit. This is because
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

is four-dimensional while all the other
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

states hold one qubit data. This holds true not just for the initial time step, but for all the time steps when
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

is the active state. Secondly, observe that whenever
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

is the active state, all the shapes in the (
qudits
) string are the states that carry qubit data. And since the number of qubits is
n^{\prime}
, we can only ever have the block length of size
n^{\prime}-1
because whenever
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

is the active state,
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

carries two qubit data.
The left flag,
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

, and the turn flag,
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

, carry non-qubit data, and that has to be compensated with another qubit carrying state/shape. Thus, increasing the block length by 1.

\blacksquare

Observe, for example, that we can ensure that the
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

is always at the end of the chain by a 2-local check shown in the third-to-last row of Table
4
. This is done by only ever allowing
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

to the left of
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

(both with and without boundary).
Note, however, that we may have configurations that can’t be checked by local projections. For example, configurations having more than the expected length of a qudit string, where the qudit string is the configuration of shapes with only dead states on its left and unborn states on its right. Consider having
\hbox to31.4pt{\vbox to16.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.69968pt\lower-8.07744pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

. This could happen in either first or the second step (when the active site is
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

), thus leading to the qudit string of length
n^{\prime}
on the grid. A qudit string of length
n^{\prime}
shall correspond to
n^{\prime}+1
qubits in the original verification circuit. And this is a contradiction since the original circuit had exactly
n^{\prime}
qubits. To remedy this, one might imagine directly penalizing

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.66667pt}{-2.6pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -16.14 -3.6)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{

. But note that this configuration is a legal one in other steps. For example, in Steps
5-8
. To penalize such illegal configurations with incorrect length of the qudit string, one needs to count the number of sites not having a
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

or a
\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

. And it is impossible to count locally (without any memory). We will now show that such configurations that do not admit a local check are exactly those violating the legal lengths of qudit strings in Fact
21
.

Claim 22

(Exceptions)
.

The only illegal configurations not penalized by the
H_{\rm Penalty}

are the (
qudits
) string violating length of a legal (
qudits
) string in Fact
21
.

Proof.

While Fact
21
dictates the correct lengths for a given active state, it could also occur that despite having the correct length, we may still have an illegal configuration by being misaligned at the block boundaries but the rules for
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\Rrightarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

and
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

in Table
4
ensure that this does not happen. For the remaining two active states, the boundary alignment problem is non-existent as the the chain can never be aligned with a block boundary. This is because when the active state is either
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\twoheadleftarrow$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

or
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

, the length of any (
qudits
) string is always
n^{\prime}
, while the length of the chain between two boundaries in
n^{\prime}-1
. Hence, the (
qudits
) string with the remaining two active states will always be bigger than the length of the block boundary.

Now observe that each state (active or not) has prohibited states on its left and right, both with and without boundary in Table
4
. Hence, any illegal state that occurs next to any (active or non-active) state can be ruled out by a local check. Thus, the only states that remain are therefore exactly the states in Fact
21
.

\blacksquare

Fact 23

(Single active site)
.

Any (
qudits
) string not penalized by
H_{\rm Penalty}
has exactly one active site.

Proof.

Note that the legal states do not evolve to illegal ones and vice-versa. Legal states only evolve to legal states because the transition rules are unique and only map legal states to legal states. To evolve one active site in time or into another active site, it is necessary to have a two-local legal configuration for the (unique) propagation rule to apply to the concerned site. Every propagation rule either changes the position of the active site or delete the current active site in the qudit string by replacing it with a different active site. Hence, preserving the number of the active sites.

\blacksquare

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

24
which ensures that
H_{\rm Penalty}+H_{\rm Prop}
have large energy in the subspace of non-locally checkable states, which shall be useful for proving soundness in the proof of
Theorem

2
.

Lemma 24

(Subspace of illegal states)
.

The minimum eigenvalue of
H_{\rm Penalty}+H_{\rm Prop}
in the subspace of illegal states without a local check as listed in Claim
22
is
\Omega(1/T^{3})
, where
T
is the total number of steps in our one-dimensional construction.

Proof.

Firstly, note that the states in Claim
22
are the states without a local check since all the others are exhaustively ruled out by our local rules. By Fact
23
, there is always a single, unique active state even in the (
qudits
) string of incorrect length. For every active state, depending on the configuration, we either have a local penalty or a propagation rule. If we had a local penalty, then then it would have been penalized by the penalty Hamiltonian. Otherwise, we will have a configuration that fits one of our propagation rules. We shall proceed by considering two cases: when the active site is
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

; and when active site is not
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

.

If the active site is
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

, then in at most
n^{\prime}-1
steps it will either hit the left or right block boundary by backward and forward propagation rules. Consequently, it will be locally penalized because only
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

and
\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

are allowed across the left and right boundary, respectively. Thus, the number of steps to evolve to a locally detectable state from any active state are at most
O(n^{\prime}-1)
. Moreover, the length of the (
qudits
) string could be at most
(n^{\prime}-1)\cdot R
, where
R
is the number of rounds/layers in our modified circuit. Thus, we encounter at least one locally detectable state in
O((n^{\prime})^{2}\cdot R)
. Thus the fraction of illegal states not admitting a local check is
\Omega(1/(n^{\prime})^{2}\cdot R)
when the the active state is
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

.

If the active state is any state other than
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

, we claim that the same scaling holds. To see this, write the length of the (
qudits
) string,
N
, as
N=(n^{\prime}-1)m+p
, where
0\leq p\leq n^{\prime}-1
and
1\leq m\leq R
. Regardless of the active state, the propagation rules guarantee that we reach
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

in
O(N\cdot(n^{\prime}-1))+O(N\cdot(n^{\prime}-1))+O(1)
, where each summand denotes the number of time steps for each of the
\hbox to31.4pt{\vbox to15.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.69968pt\lower-7.97745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -21.45 -10.76 h 42.89 v 21.52 h -42.89 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-12.16667pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -16.84 -3.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\hbox to31.4pt{\vbox to15.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.69968pt\lower-7.97745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -21.45 -10.76 h 42.89 v 21.52 h -42.89 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-12.16667pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -16.84 -3.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
and
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

, respectively, to change into the next active state.
Therefore, it takes a maximum of
O((n^{\prime})^{2}R)
steps to reach
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

and another
O(n^{\prime})
from there to reach a locally detectable state at the block boundary. This implies that we have at least
\Omega(1/((n^{\prime})^{2}R)
states that are locally checkable. As can be seen in Fig.
4
, the total number of steps
T=O((n^{\prime})^{2}R)
, implying that the fraction of locally checkable illegal states is
\Omega(1/T)
, which also lower bounds
\sin^{2}(\theta/2)
in Lemma
17
.
Finally, plugging in the bound on the minimum non-zero eigenvalue of
H_{\rm Prop}=\Omega(1/T^{2})
from
Ref.
[
24
]
in Lemma
17
, we get that

\lambda_{\min}(H_{\rm Prop}+H_{\rm Penalty})=\Omega(1/T^{3}),

(102)

which proves the claim.

\blacksquare

Note that we have already taken care of the cases when the states are illegal — both with and without a local check. The only remaining case is that of the legal states. At this point, the proof of hardness is straightforward.

3.3
MA-hardness proof

The full Hamiltonian
H=H_{\rm Init}+H_{\rm Prop}/2+H_{\rm Penalty}+H_{\rm Final}
in one-dimension is defined similarly to the 2D case.
H_{\rm Init}
and
H_{\rm Final}
are also constructed similarly and are again stoquastic since they only comprise of projections.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

First, note that with
H_{\rm Init}
and
H_{\rm Final}
analogously as in Theorem
1
the completeness analysis follows directly from
Section

2.5.1
. For soundness, we will argue that regardless of the type of subspace, legal or illegal (locally checkable or not), the minimum eigenvalue of
H=H_{\rm Init}+H_{\rm Prop}/2+H_{\rm Penalty}+H_{\rm Final}
is at least
1/T^{3}
, where
T
is the total number of time steps in the verification circuit. Firstly, if we are in a locally checkable configuration, then
\lambda_{\min}(H_{\rm Penalty})\geq 1
since
H_{\rm Penalty}
is diagonal in the basis of locally checkable illegal states. Since all other Hamiltonian terms are positive semi-definite, the
\Omega(1/T^{3})
bound holds. For the legal states, the bound still holds as shown in Section
2.5.2
. For non-locally checkable configurations, thanks to
Lemma

24
, the
\Omega(1/T^{3})
continues to hold. Finally, the particles in the one-dimensional case are 19-dimensional because
\hskip 5.5pt{\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

,
\hskip 5.5pt{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\times$}}\bigcirc}\hskip 5.5pt

and
\hskip 5.5pt\circlearrowleft\hskip 5.5pt

are one-dimensional each.
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Updownarrow$}}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{\raisebox{0.35004pt}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}}\bigcirc}

and
{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\uparrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\downarrow$}}\bigcirc}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\rightarrow$}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{$\leftarrow$}}\bigcirc}

are four-dimensional each, while the remaining four particles are two-dimensional each. The total dimension is simply the sum of each dimension since the particle Hilbert space is a direct sum of each of these configurations.

\blacksquare

4
StoqMA-completeness

Bravyi, Bessen and Terhal
[
9
]
have introduced a new class, referred to as
StoqMA, and showed that stoquastic LH-MIN is a complete problem for the class StoqMA. Recall that StoqMA is a class that allows the verification circuit to only have classically reversible gates followed by measurements only in the
\{\left|+\right\rangle,\left|-\right\rangle\}
basis. This basis restriction for the measurement does not allow for the exponential amplification/suppression of completeness/soundness parameters. To ensure an inverse polynomial gap between the eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian corresponding to yes and no instances, the authors in Ref.
[
9
]
used first order time-independent perturbation theory. They divide the full Hamiltonian into the unperturbed part and the perturbation Hamiltonian — which they take to be
H_{\rm Final}

6
6

6

The construction in Ref.
[
9
]
does not have the
H_{\rm Clock}
term. Nevertheless, the same lower bound holds even with the
H_{\rm Clock}
. See Lemma 3.11 in
Ref.
[
6
]
.
. To ensure that this “perturbation" was small, as is required for the perturbative approach to work, they scaled
H_{\rm Final}
by a parameter
\delta\ll\Omega(1/T^{3})
, where
\Omega(1/T^{3})
is the lower bound on the spectral gap
7
7

7

The spectral gap is the difference between the smallest and second smallest eigenvalue.
of

A_{1}\coloneqq H_{\rm Init}+H_{\rm Prop}.

(103)

The spectral analysis is borrowed from Lemma 3.11
[
6
]
by setting
s=1
therein.

Corollary 25

(StoqMA-completeness)
.

Geometrically two-local stoquastic LH-MIN is StoqMA-complete in two and one dimensions with 14 and 19 state particles, respectively.

Proof.

The proof almost exactly follows from Appendix. 3 in Ref.
[
9
]
. Let
A_{1}\coloneqq H_{\rm Init}+H_{\rm Prop}+H_{\rm Clock}
and the perturbation Hamiltonian to be
A_{2}=\delta H_{\rm Out}
where
\delta\ll 1/T^{3}
. Now, compared to the setting of Ref.
[
9
]
, we only have an additional
H_{\rm Clock}
term in the “unperturbed” Hamiltonian,
A_{1}
, that has the valid history state as its zero eigenstate.
So the completeness analysis in Ref.
[
9
]
still holds. Soundness also simply follows since
\left\langle\psi\right|H_{\rm Clock}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 0
for any quantum state vector
\left|\psi\right\rangle
. The desired statement follows by noting that these two properties of
H_{\rm Clock}
hold in both our 2D and one-dimensional constructions.

\blacksquare

5
Conclusion and future work

To understand the computational complexity
of physically meaningful
families of Hamiltonians is one of the core aims of the field of
Hamiltonian complexity, located at the intersection of
theoretical computer science and quantum many-body physics.
In this work, we studied the hardness of geometric stoquastic local Hamiltonian problem in two natural readings, for a two-dimensional grid and on a one-dimensional line. For
both settings, we prove that it remains MA-hard if the particles have sufficiently high local dimension. From a Physics perspective, this may be seen as a surprising result: After all, such systems are substantially closer to the reality of physically plausible sign-free Hamiltonians, and hence
this maintained hardness may come as a surprise. Also, the sign-free geometrically local
Hamiltonians are commonly considered the “easy”
instances of Hamiltonians from the perspective of Monte Carlo sampling, so viewed from this perspective, it may be surprising to see that the approximation
of their ground state energy is MA-hard. It may be worth noting that the general question of the computational complexity of bringing geometrically local Hamiltonians into
a manifestly stoquastic form is still open.

We think that one can further reduce the dimension of the particles in the two-dimensional construction by using the ideas from the one-dimensional construction, and hence
bring the families of systems considered closer to plausible models
from the condensed matter physics context: Only having the primary working qubit (the active state) to be of high enough dimension. Alternatively, only applying two qubit gates would also significantly reduce particle dimension. For that to work, it would be imperative to find two qubit gates that are classically reversible, universal and preserve stoquasticity in the construction.

It would also be interesting to
bring this type of study into contact with efforts of understanding
the computational complexity of
two-local qubit Hamiltonians

[
12
]
. Indeed,
arguably, the physically best motivated and theoretically interesting is the case where the particle dimension is
d=2
, i.e., the qubit case. We expect that stoquastic perturbation gadgets would be useful to address the qubit case. Another
presumably “easier” version of the problem is the translationally invariant case
[
21
]
, where all the local Hamiltonian terms are the same on all sites. It would be exciting to see if the problem persists to be MA
{}_{\text{EXP}}
-hard
8
8

8

In the translationally invariant setting, the input is the number of particles
n
, which requires
\log n
bits to specify. Thus any polynomial-in-
n
algorithm is exponential in the input size.
in the translationally invariant setting, again closer to physical reality. One could also think of the complexity of the
variant of the problem in which
certain qubits are pinned
[
26
]
,
where pinning refers to fixing the state of a small subsystem.
On a higher level, it is important to bring results in Hamiltonian complexity closer to the physical reality of natural quantum many-body systems. It is the hope that the present work that takes new
steps in this direction stimulates further such efforts along these lines.

6
